<note>catchword: my</note>

“Who will bring you the Sacrament? Indeed, they will not even deem you worthy to be buried in the churchyard among other pious Christians. Oh, what great shame and disgrace you will bring upon your dear parents and our entire lineage!”

**AUD.** Dear brothers, learn to know Jesus Christ: the crucified, the despised, and the scorned. Then you would have no need at all for this unnecessary fear and worry. Whoever does not wish to be despised and scorned for Christ’s sake is also deprived of Christ’s death and does not know what Baptism and the Lord’s Supper truly are. We must die with Christ, and also rise and live with Him.

**PAULUS. JOHANNES.** See, you utter such strange speech and bring forth unusual things, the likes of which we have not heard from our dear pastor <term>Seelsorger; literally "soul-carer," a traditional German term for a parish priest</term> in all our lives, nor read in any book. Therefore, you will be denounced as a fanatic <term>Schwermer; a derogatory label for "enthusiasts" or radical reformers who claimed direct spiritual revelation</term> or an Anabaptist <term>Widertäuffer; "rebaptizer," a group often persecuted and socially marginalized during this period</term>.

**AUD.** You are my dear brothers, my blood, my flesh. I wish as much for you as for myself, if only you would accept it: that through the anointing <note>Refers to the "unction" or inner teaching of the Holy Spirit (1 John 2:27)</note> within you, you might learn to know and love only Jesus Christ the crucified. You would rejoice with me and not mourn for me; the peace of Christ is in me. I have the Priest within me; He hears my confession <note>original: "Beichte"</note>, He absolves me <note>original: "Absolvirt"</note>, He feeds me, He makes me a true, living member of the Catholic Church <note>The speaker uses "Catholic" in its original sense of "Universal," referring to the spiritual body of all believers rather than the Roman Catholic institution</note>. It matters nothing to me what men judge of me, so long as I am in Jesus Christ and also die in Him.

**PAUL. JOHAN.** Can we obtain nothing from you? Look at our honorable names which we possess, and yet we must be reviled for your sake, so that people say: “This one has fallen away from the Catholic Church; he has become a fanatic and a heretic <note>original: "Ketzer"</note> in his teaching; he does not keep to the established doctrines of the land.”

**AUD.** Ah brothers, brothers, you cause yourselves much unrest where it is not needed. What is honor and a name before the world? A base, fleeting thing. It is honor before God that should gladden us, and not the shameful honor among men. Whoever loves Christ knows well that I am neither a fanatic nor a heretic, and that...

# Christian Dialogue

L iij

<note>original: "mein Name..." — This continues the sentence from the previous page: "...and not mourn for me, but rather rejoice that] my name is written in heaven."</note>

...that my name is written in heaven. Oh, if only you would strive for the Book of Life and allow yourselves to be written into it, then no shame, mockery, or dishonor could ever stand against you.

**DEATH.** <note>original: "MORS"</note> You certainly recognize that even your brothers do not wish to know or accept Christ. And just as your preacher <term>Concionator; Latin for a public speaker or preacher</term> led you away from Christ throughout his life, so too would your brothers gladly mislead and seduce you. But he who hates the world—and likewise father, mother, brother, sister, wife, child, and even his own life—he is the one who truly knows and loves the LORD Christ.

**AUDITOR.** <note>Auditus</note> The anointing <note>Refers to the "unction" of the Holy Spirit (1 John 2:27), a key concept in mystical Christianity where God teaches the believer directly</note> and holy Baptism testify to me that I, and every believer, must necessarily die to three things while still in my healthy life:

First, to the world, with its lust, joy, and pleasure.

Second, to the love of men, so that out of love for Christ I regard as nothing my father, mother, wife, child, brother, and sister.

Third, I must also give up my soul, my own life, which is dearer and more beloved to me than the world and all my joy.

**PAULUS and JOHANNES.** Yes, you traffic in such speeches which are not customary in this land <note>original: "nicht Landbräuchig"</note>, and which have been used and practiced by the Anabaptists <term>Widertäuffer; "rebaptizers," a radical Reformation group often viewed as social and religious revolutionaries</term> and other heretics <term>Ketzer</term>.

**AUDITOR.** I tell you, my brothers, if you do not learn to deny and lose these three things, you cannot know, have, or keep Christ; you will not be able to be members of the true Church of God. Ultimately, in the final hour, you must leave these three things behind in misery and distress anyway. You weep over me because they call me a fanatic <term>Schwermer; a derogatory label for those claiming direct spiritual inspiration</term> and a heretic, and because they cast me out of their church and will perhaps even deny me a burial after my death. But I weep over you, because you do not know Christ Jesus, and you remain outside of the heavenly citizenship <term>Himmlische Burgerschafft; a reference to Philippians 3:20, implying that one's true home and legal standing is in heaven</term>, even if your confession is heard, you are absolved, and you are buried in the churchyard—yes, even in the church itself.

<summary>Auditus explains the "three-fold death" to the world, family, and self required for true faith, while his brothers accuse him of adopting the dangerous rhetoric of heretical sects. Auditus counters that social and religious rituals, like formal burial or absolution, are worthless without a true internal knowledge of Christ.</summary>

### Chapter Eight.

#### Auditus dies without a priest, confession, absolution, or the Sacrament, and is buried in a field; his brothers Paulus and Johannes are deeply grieved, but Death comforts them.

**JOHANNES.** Dear brother, you are becoming very weak; you should reconcile yourself with your God and make your confession <term>Beichte; the formal practice of confessing sins to a priest</term> so that you may die like any other pious Christian.

**AUDITUS.** <note>The name Auditus means "The Hearer."</note> If I am not already reconciled with God through faith in the death of Christ—who is my life—then my reconciliation through confession will surely be in vain. I am anxious, I am in pain; do not cause me any more unrest.

**PAULUS.** Then at least let us fetch the Deacon and receive the Sacrament <term>the Lord’s Supper, often given as "Last Rites"</term> for the forgiveness of your sins, so that you may be helped. For from that you must draw your comfort.

**AUDITUS.** Oh, do not torment me so in my great agony.

**JOHANNES.** We have warned and pleaded with you faithfully enough that you should step away from your stubborn opinion and follow the teaching of your dear pastor <term>Seelsorger; literally "soul-caretaker," the local minister</term>. Yet you would not listen to us; you remain in your fanaticism <term>Schwermerey; a derogatory term used by the established church to describe radical or mystical believers</term>. You create for yourself a peculiar faith contrary to the nature and custom of the entire Holy Roman Empire; now you might well despair of God.

**AUDITUS.** Oh, where shall I go? My sins are heavy and exceedingly great; who will deliver me from the body of this death? <note>original: "wer wil mich doch erlösen von dem Leibe dieses Todts?" — a quote from Romans 7:24</note>

**PAULUS.** Do not lose heart; comfort yourself in the LORD Christ, in His death and His merit. Take His words to heart: "For God so loved the world" <note>John 3:16</note>; "Christ is my life" <note>Philippians 1:21</note>, etc. And do not trouble yourself with your sins, even if you have erred in faith or doctrine. Think of the beautiful example of our dear pastor, who also knew how to comfort himself well: no sin is so great that God’s grace is not even greater.

**JOHANNES.** If your faith is righteous, as you have always persisted in your opinion, then take comfort in your good conscience. But if it was false, and you loved false doctrine, then simply perform penance <term>Buß; can mean both inner repentance and formal acts of penance</term> and confess it before the priest; he will forgive you everything in God’s stead. For his forgiveness is the forgiveness of GOD the Almighty LORD...

**JOHANNES.** He made a fine confession to us, comforted himself well with the Scripture verses, and passed away gently in strong faith, without any temptation <note>original: "Anfechtung," referring to spiritual trials or the internal assaults of the devil</note> or any despair.

**DEACON.** It cannot be otherwise; you will obtain nothing from the Bishop. In his life, he separated himself from our faith; it is only just that he now be separated from the Holy Christian community in death.

**PAULUS.** Why should we linger long with the corpse? It will begin to smell. Our pleading is all in vain anyway. We will have him carried out on a wagon, as no one will want to carry him.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 88. Johannes is protesting the Deacon's order to bury his brother in an ignominious location. The character "MORS" (Death) appears here as a participant in the dialogue, acting as a spiritual teacher who challenges the reliance on outward rituals and social status.</meta>

### Christian Dialogue

...also be buried in the field.

**JOHANNES.** If we were not able to pray the Lord’s Prayer <term>the "Our Father"</term> and did not know Christ, then we would be erring in the faith and entirely uncertain of our salvation.

**MORS <note>Latin for "Death"; here personified as a theological interlocutor</note>.** Whoever understands the Lord’s Prayer and the third article of the Creed <term>the section of the Apostles' Creed concerning the Holy Spirit, the Church, and the forgiveness of sins</term> clearly recognizes that every believer is within the Christian Church and possesses the communion of all heavenly goods within himself. Such a person does not need to wait for an outward absolution <term>formal forgiveness</term> from a priest, nor does he need to seek comfort or the forgiveness of sins from the Sacrament <note>the Eucharist</note>. He who does not have confession and absolution within himself through faith in Christ is not helped at all by confession and absolution from a priest. For the unrepentant, the "mouth-absolution" <note>original: "Maul absolviren"; meaning a purely verbal or ritualistic forgiveness without inner heart change</note> is of no use, even if he receives it; and for the believer, it does no harm even if he does not have it. To the unbeliever, the Sacrament gives nothing, even if he receives it; and for the believer, it does no harm if he is unable to receive it. He who has died in faith is not harmed even if he were buried under the gallows <note>a place for executed criminals, the most shameful burial site</note>. And he who has died in unbelief without repentance is not helped by a burial in the churchyard. Everything depends on the inner man, upon whom alone God looks.

**PAULUS.** I have not heard such things from our pastor; they seem to be true and might be acceptable. But our honorable name would be slandered if our brother were not to be buried among the other Christians. Our concern is for our honorable lineage, and not for his soul; we are now unable to save that.

**MORS.** There you both reveal your unbelief, your unrepentant life, and your ignorance, showing that you have never truly known Christ. For if you did, you would not cling to your good name, nor would you regard your honorable lineage so highly; shame, scorn, and mockery would taste better to you than an honorable name and great honor before men. You must deny and lose everything if you wish to be found in Christ through faith—the entire world with all its pleasures, and thereafter <unclear>ver-</unclear> <note>catchword: "ver-", likely leading to "verachten" (despise) or "verlieren" (lose)</note>

<summary>This page presents a radical spiritualist argument that internal faith and the "inner man" are more important than outward rituals like priestly absolution, the Eucharist, or burial in consecrated ground. Mors (Death) rebukes Johannes and Paulus for prioritizing their family's social reputation over true spiritual understanding.</summary>

But whoever does not understand these things nor lives in them has neither faith nor Christ within him. He has a heavy, unbearable yoke, and after his death, he journeys into unrest and darkness, even if he had confessed to the priest and received absolution with the Sacrament <note>the Eucharist or Holy Communion</note> from him.

**JOHANNES.** From these words, we should almost gain a good hope. Let us walk together into the field and ponder this further; it could well be that our brother did not fare so badly after all.

### The Ninth Chapter.

The Preacher <note>original: "Conc." or "Concionator," Latin for a preacher or minister</note> appears to the brothers in the field / testifies alongside Death that he is in darkness. **JOHANNES.** What comes this way? It has a form like our Preacher; it is the evil spirit / he has taken his form upon himself / he wants to deceive us / may God protect us from Satan.

**PREACHER.** Alas, it is I myself and not Satan / I was your pastor in the world / but I led you wrongly / turn away from my writings and books / inform the others as well / follow your brother who sits in rest and in the light / but I am driven around in the darkness.

**DEATH <note>original: "MORS"</note>.** Do not fear / it is no evil spirit / it is he himself, just as he said. **DEATH.** No one comes into the darkness unless they lived in darkness on earth, and no one comes into the light unless they walked in the light on earth, which is in Christ. The blessed have heaven within them and are in heaven. The damned have hell within them and are in hell.

### Christian Dialogue

The essential, bodily union is manifest, both in this world and the next.

**PAULUS.** What form does it take then in that world?

**DEATH.** I am a master and steward in both. One world is within the other: those who are in that world have lost the body and all physicality. There is neither earth nor water, neither sun nor moon, neither foliage nor grass. They see nothing physical, they have nothing physical, nothing carries them, nothing lifts them; they only hover; nothing physical hinders them. It seems to them as if this physical world were no longer there, and yet it is still there. Just as it seems to you physical humans that the invisible world is not there, and yet it is there; you physical ones do not see the spiritual, and yet it is there; and the spiritual ones do not see the physical, and yet it is there.

**PREACHER.** <note>original: "CONC.", short for Concionator</note> Those of us who live in that world are beside you, around you, but we do not see your body, do not hear your voice; it is as if we were separated from you by many German miles <note>A German mile was a historical unit of distance much longer than a standard English mile, often around 4.6 miles.</note>; this is caused only by <term>spirituality</term> <note>original: "Geistligkeit"</note> and <term>physicality</term> <note>original: "Leibligkeit"</note>. For we in our world do not have physical eyes, nor ears in a visible way. Therefore, the sun and moon are of no use to us; we count neither hours, nor months, weeks nor years, and are entirely separated from you, and yet are still around you.

**DEATH.** This visible world is round and enclosed with the outer <term>testa</term> <note>Latin for "shell"; used here to describe the hard outer boundary of the physical universe.</note> or shell of the <term>firmament</term> <note>In historical cosmology, the firmament was the vast, solid dome of the sky that separated the earth from the heavens.</note>. Outside of this circle there is no creature. The damned and the blessed are within the circle, and each is within the other, and yet they are separated. To you physical ones, it seems as if the spiritual inner world were not there, and yet it is beside you, indeed, through you. And it seems to the spiritual ones that this visible world is no longer there, and they are yet still within it.

### THE AUTHOR OF THE DIALOGUE TO THE
->Reader.<-

This dialogue / favorable / kindhearted and impartial reader / is not written out of a curiosity to make books / or to hunt for worldly fame among men / but it was written for this very reason / that the pious here and there throughout the whole world might be confirmed / in the recognized truth. The unbelievers, however, remain excluded from the Holy Catholic <note>The author uses "Catholic" here in its original sense of "universal," referring to the spiritual community of all Christians rather than the specific Roman Catholic denomination.</note> Church. For this is the judgment or sentence / that the light comes into the world / to make a distinction between the children of light / who receive it / and between the children of darkness / who reject the book and the light / and remain in the works of darkness. For upon the pulpits one is not allowed to preach Christ Crucified <note>original: "Christum Crucifixum"</note> / he is not tolerated in all lands / as spiritual eyes can well see / so I have nevertheless considered it necessary to do and confess such things in writing. You, my reader, apply diligence / so that this may be prepared for printing / no one will accept it but the Christians / and no one will be offended by it but the unbelievers. Should anything be found too harsh or not in accordance with the truth / I ask you as an impartial reader and brother / that you would refute and explain such things out of love / so that I and others may come and remain on the right path / it shall [be to] me

# Christian Dialogue

<meta>catchword: know/</meta>

Here / nature itself gives you to recognize / that in death lies the greatest fruit / a grain of wheat <note>original Latin: "granum tritici"</note>, if it dies / it brings a beautiful stalk with 20 or 30 grains, many from one <note>original Latin: "ex uno plurima"</note>, this you indeed cannot deny / if one cuts a snake into 5 or 6 pieces / and places them into putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the stage of decay in alchemy where matter is broken down to be reborn</term> in their field / 6 snakes become from one. Thus one can bring a tree back into its youth. See folio 73 of the recorded Metamorphoses.

**DEATH.** The Listener is better taught than you and all your crowd / for he knows the life / that must come out of death / just as you also should rightly know Christ / to go with Him into death / to die / so that you would also come into a life. But you do not care much for Christ / you preach Him with your mouth / yet your conduct is entirely against Him / nor can you tolerate any man who belongs to Christ / who teaches rightly of death and life.

**LISTENER.** I thank God / that I recognize in the Light of Nature <note>A common concept in Renaissance philosophy referring to knowledge gained through observing the natural world rather than through scripture alone</note> the greatest and highest mystery / namely death and life / whereby a thing is destroyed / killed / and becomes nothing in its first form / and afterward becomes much nobler in its form / power / and virtue / than it was before. I wish to prove such a thing in the Divine Art of Alchemy <note>original: "Alchimia"</note> alone / that through death

the noblest and best life comes forth.

**LISTENER.** All people and all stations of life naturally desire two things: namely, a healthy life and wealth. All people have sought such things since the beginning of the world; but I speak of wealth, not that one gathers goods unjustly, without burdening or harming anyone; such a thing cannot be attained unless one learns to rightly recognize the new birth through death, both in the Heavenly and natural light.

**DEATH.** There are many ways to gather wealth and goods: one through the oppression and burdening of the poor; another through robbing, stealing, and fraud; the third through usury and immoral dealings, all of which are against Christ. But this your Listener, who knows me well, speaks of such treasures of wealth that can be attained without any harm to one's neighbor, and also without any sin, through rebirth alone <note>original Latin: "per solam regenerationem"</note>.

# Christian Dialogue

...air and fire were forbidden. **DEATH.** I have seen that in many places—that all who are mine are received in such a way. **PREACHER.** I would very much like to see, know, and hear how one might come to wealth through this "new birth," so that I would not need to remain a hireling <note>A "Mietling" is a biblical reference (John 10:12) to a shepherd who works only for pay and has no real care for the sheep</note> and preach for praise <note>original: "Loben," though contextually this often refers to "Lohn" or wages</note>, etc.

### On True Christianity

...writes thus: A jewel of my treasure lies to be found in Friuli <note>A region in northeastern Italy, often mentioned in alchemical texts of this period</note> (he speaks of alchemy, which you do not understand) that you, German Charles and Roman Leo <note>A reference to Emperor Charles V and Pope Leo X, the secular and ecclesiastical heads of the Western world during the early Reformation</note>, with all your wealth cannot pay for; and this poverty your **LISTENER** also possesses through divine granting. **PREACHER.** It would be Christ; He is my treasure, as well as the treasure of others; the whole world cannot pay me for this, let alone Caesar and the Pope <note>original: "Cæsar & Papa"</note>. **DEATH.** Your Listener is not speaking right now of the eternal heavenly treasure, which all believers have regardless; he is speaking now of the earthly, temporal treasure of the world, which he possesses and understands, and holds in Christ Jesus. **PREACHER.** If many a Count or Prince possessed such a thing, he would not shear his subjects so with unbearable burdens. Dear friend, tell me a little how one comes by it.

# Christian Dialogue

...it is despised; no one knows it except the wise. But should there also be such a thing in Nature? If it is to be a life for all **metals**, it must first die and then come into life. **DEATH.** See to it that you do not reveal everything! People might seek it out of a feverish passion; you have already done too much by revealing the **character** <note>original: "Cheracter," likely referring to a specific alchemical symbol or the "signature" of the matter</note>. **LISTENER.** All the wise keep the thing secret; but I speak of it in such a way that no one on earth may learn from it unless he hears it from God in Christ Jesus—be he noble or common, prince or peasant. **PREACHER.** Although I have no hope of attaining it, I would still like to hear more of it for the sake of the wonder of it. **LISTENER.** The thing is dead; it must come into a new life. If it is to come into a new life, it must die, and through its own life become living again. **PREACHER.** How might that happen? **LISTENER.** Just as the other birth of man cannot be renewed or made living through foreign things, so it is with the thing of which I speak: it has its life within itself, but is dead. See the rest in *Searchings* <note>original: "Suchten," likely referring to a specific book or chapter title</note>, folio 4, etc.

<meta>This page continues the "Christian Dialogue" between the Listener, the Preacher, and Death. # Of True Christianity.

### Death: A Conclusion for All the Living.

I am named **Death**; no one looks upon me gladly, yet I am among all, around all, and through all, a mighty and powerful Lord over everything that lives above the firmament. Therefore, you **Listener** <note>original Latin: "Auditor"</note> and **Preacher** <note>original Latin: "Concionator"</note>—that is, you of the spiritual and worldly estates—take note of what I and the writer of this dialogue say. There are **three things** <note>In mystical theology, this often refers to the renunciation of the world, the flesh, and the devil, or the surrender of one's own will, desire, and ego.</note> that you, the heads of the world along with your subjects, must learn to lose and renounce during your physical lives. If you do not do this, you do not accept Christ; you do not remain in Him, nor He in you. Furthermore, you do not allow **GOD** to draw or teach you through His **holy inspiration** <term>Inspiration: literally "in-speaking" (einsprechen), the internal voice or guidance of the Holy Spirit within the soul</term>. Therefore, I come to everyone and take these things from you by force, and send each one where he belongs. There it will cause him pain and eternal regret that he was ever a human being on earth. 

But if you are obedient to Christ and learn to lose these three things during your physical life with a good will, then you remain in **Love**—that is, in God—and God in you. God teaches you daily and gives you good admonition through His holy inspiration to think and do what is right; you read this dialogue with joy and promote its printing to admonish others more and to awaken them through it. And when I come to you, you will have an unburdened conscience to depart from this world in **Jesus Christ**; you will rejoice that you were born and lived as humans on earth. May this be given and granted to you by **Him who is, and who was, and who is to come** <note>A reference to Revelation 1:8, emphasizing the eternal nature of God.</note>, Amen, etc.

<summary>Death delivers a stern warning to both clergy and laity, explaining that unless one willingly renounces worldly attachments during life, Death will take them by force, leading to eternal regret. Conversely, those who submit to God's inner teaching find peace and joy in the face of mortality.</summary>
<keywords>Death, True Christianity, holy inspiration, renunciation, conscience, Jesus Christ, spiritual vs worldly estates</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the preceding "Christian Dialogue" between the Listener, the Preacher, and Death into a series of philosophical and hagiographic anecdotes. While the previous page focused on the spiritual necessity of renouncing the world and preparing for the finality of Death's arrival, this page provides historical examples of "wise men" who viewed death with indifference or joy, illustrating the "sweet sleep" mentioned in the title.</meta>

# Christian Dialogue

### From the Lives of the Fathers <note>original Latin: "Ex vitis Patrum." This refers to a famous collection of stories and sayings from the Desert Fathers, the early Christian hermits and monks of the 4th and 5th centuries.</note>: That Death is a Sweet Sleep.

When once an old, wise master lay dying and was about to depart from this world, his disciples stood sorrowfully around him and wept. But he cast up his eyes and laughed, and he did this three times in succession. Then they asked him what the cause was that he laughed so, while they were weeping. He spoke to them earnestly: "I laughed the first time because you are afraid of death. The second time I laughed because you are not prepared for dying. The third time I laughed because I am going out of unrest and labor, into death toward rest, peace, and life." Should I then not laugh while you weep? And as he had said this, he departed from here.

When **Diogenes** <note>Diogenes of Sinope, the most famous of the Cynic philosophers, known for his extreme asceticism and social critiques.</note> was asked by his friends where they should bury him, he said: "One should let me lie unburied and place a staff beside me, so that I might drive the birds and wild animals away from myself." They spoke: "How can you do that, since you are dead and feel nothing?" He said: "What then would the bite of animals harm me, since I no longer perceive anything?"

When **Anaxagoras** <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who brought philosophy to Athens.</note> was told that his son had died, he said: "I knew well beforehand that he had to die, from the moment he was born."

When **Lysimachus** the Tyrant threatened **Theodorus of Cyrene** <note>A Greek philosopher of the Cyrenaic school who was known for his atheism and indifference to external circumstances.</note> that he wanted to crucify him, he replied: "In such a way you may make your courtiers fearful." **Theodorus** asked nothing further of it, whether he rotted in the air or in the earth.

So one also reads of the **Cynic** <note>referring again to the school of Cynicism, which taught that the purpose of life is to live in virtue, in agreement with nature, and with total indifference to conventional desires or burial rites.</note>, when he was asked how he wished to be buried. He spoke: "O dear ones, be unconcerned about that; the stench and the animals will surely bury me." But it is not fine that such a man's flesh should be eaten by animals. "What can that harm," he spoke, "if I am of use to no one after death?"

<summary>A collection of anecdotes from the Desert Fathers and Greek philosophers (Diogenes, Anaxagoras, Theodorus) illustrating a courageous and indifferent attitude toward death and burial. These stories serve as moral examples of viewing death as a peaceful transition or a matter of physical insignificance.</summary>

<keywords>Death, burial, Desert Fathers, Diogenes, Anaxagoras, Theodorus of Cyrene, Cynicism, stoicism, afterlife, mortality</keywords>

## Table of Contents or Order of the Chapters in
->this Dialogue.<-

### The First Chapter.
The Listener <note>In the German "Zuhörer," often paired with "Auditor" in the Latinate style of the time, referring to the seeker or student.</note> rejoices to be essentially united with God through Christ, which the Preacher <note>original: "Predicant"</note> recognizes and judges to be an error. **11**

### The Second Chapter.
The Listener disputes with the Preacher <note>original: "Concionatore," another term for a minister or orator.</note> concerning the correct understanding of **imputed righteousness** <note>original: "imputativæ justitiæ," the theological doctrine that a believer is declared righteous by God through faith in Christ, rather than by their own works.</note>, and how all preaching is in vain without the **Anointing** <note>original: "Salbung"</note>—that is, without the inner Word or Hearing—which the Preacher also judges and recognizes as fanaticism <note>original: "Schwermerey," a derogatory term used by established church authorities for those claiming direct spiritual revelation.</note>. **25**

### The Third Chapter.
That the mortification <note>original: "Tödtung"</note> of the flesh is necessary through the death of Christ within us, which death is our life; the Preacher, however, comforts himself with the death of Christ from the outside, through imputed righteousness. **35**

### The Fourth Chapter.
Here, Death confirms the teaching and opinion of the Listener, whereby the Preacher is convinced of the great harm that comes from the denial of the inner Word or Hearing. **50**

### The Fifth Chapter.
Here, Death, the Preacher, and the Listener speak with one another again, and it is proven that the necessary, essential indwelling of God in man is not heretical or **Osiandrian** <note>Referring to the followers of Andreas Osiander (1498–1552), a controversial theologian who argued that righteousness is the actual presence of Christ’s divine nature in the believer.</note>, but Apostolic and Prophetic. **60**

### The Sixth Chapter.
How the Cross, or the suffering and death of Christ, is emptied of its meaning and denied throughout the world, so that no one preaches Christ crucified from the pulpit; the cause of this is given as imputed righteousness from the outside <note>original: "imputativa justitia, ab extra."</note>. **72**

### The Eighth Chapter.

The **Listener** <note>original: "Auditor," the spiritual seeker.</note> dies without a priest, confession, absolution, or sacrament; he is buried in the open field <note>Being buried "in the field" rather than in a churchyard was a common historical punishment for heretics or those who refused the Church's final rites.</note>, over which his brothers **John** and **Paul** grieve very bitterly. **Death** <note>Death is personified as a character here.</note> comforts them.

### A Despised, Unacceptable Song, about the Life of Christ.

1. Oh my soul, look around you /
What Christ says, hear the same /
My yoke is easy, my burden light /
Follow me, let all things go /
You shall never regret it.

2. Oh, look around you, how many enemies there are /
Who follow this true teaching:
The whole world is entirely against Christ /
As the works clearly testify /
Defiance to whoever refutes this.

There is no love nor fear of God /
**Satan** rules everywhere /
In all offices <note>original: "Amptern," referring to both church and civic positions.</note> altogether /
Faith is strictly rejected.

4. The godless heap of the **Clergy** /
Rules over the **Laity**.
No Christian work comes from them /
They are in all things entirely against him /
Yes, they are God's worst enemies.

5. No shame, no vice or sin /
Is there that one does not find among them /
In the slightest they do not align /
With the life of Christ; how can it be /
That good should follow from it?

Nor give assent to or understand <meta>catchword: For</meta>

26. Only then would your life be ours / and your yoke would be soft and easy / when you in us / and we in you / are united and always here / holding ourselves according to your teaching.

27. For such knowledge I thank God / that he has given it to me / and I pray him that he grant me / his grace and help, so that I without fear / may walk in the life of Christ.

# A Sermon on the Rich
## Man and Poor Lazarus / how they both
### lived / died / and were buried
->Luke, Chapter 16.<-

Dear Christians / our LORD Christ teaches and admonishes us in this discourse <note>The "discourse" refers to the parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus found in Luke 16:19–31.</note> / five points / which we must and shall believe without contradiction.

Firstly, that there are two worlds / such as this present / bodily / and transitory one / where we live together in the flesh during this time. And a future spiritual world / which endures without end. Where the deceased are together / as is revealed from this discourse / that the rich man and poor Lazarus are in that other world.

Secondly / we must believe and confess from this discourse / that the other world is divided / by the great chasm <note>original: "grosse Klufft"; referring to the "great gulf fixed" that prevents passage between heaven and hell (Luke 16:26).</note> into two parts. Namely, into life / light / rest / and salvation <term>salvation: "Seligkeit," referring to a state of eternal blessedness or happiness in the presence of God</term> / in which Lazarus sits in Abraham’s bosom <note>original: "Schoß Abrahæ"; a traditional Jewish and early Christian term for the place of comfort where the righteous dead await the resurrection.</note>. And the other part into unrest / death / darkness and damnation / where the rich man sits in the torment of hell. He looks back at this world / because of the great agony he suffers / he cries out and howls / but all in vain. Lazarus, on the other hand, remains so comforted and gladdened / that he does not speak / but Abraham performs this on his behalf. For so great is the rest and salvation of the believers in Abraham’s bo-

<summary>This page introduces a sermon on the Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, establishing the reality of a spiritual afterlife divided between eternal rest and eternal torment. The author emphasizes that the two worlds are separated by an unbridgeable chasm, where the faithful find comfort and the wicked find only vain regret.</summary>

<header>A Sermon</header>

...[souls] <note>The fragment "hæ" likely completes a word like "animæ" (souls) from the preceding lost text.</note> so that they do not concern themselves with the damned. Much less should they think back on the misery or crosses they suffered. For the suffering of this time, which is temporal and slight, is not worthy of the glory that shall be revealed in the other world <note>original: "in altero mundo"</note> to the believers. Just as the pleasure and joy of the unbelievers in this time—which is also temporal and slight—is also not worthy of the great torment, pain, and hellish torture that will be revealed to the damned in that world. Abraham expresses this in his answer to the rich man, saying: "You received your good things in the world, while Lazarus conversely received evil; now he is comforted, and you are tormented." <note>A paraphrase of Luke 16:25.</note>

That the other world is divided into two parts by a great chasm <note>original: "grosse Klufft"</note> can also be observed even in this temporal, visible, bodily world. In one land, in one city, village, or indeed often in a single house, there is great tribulation and heartache, while in another there is great pleasure, joy, and delight. Yes, often at one table, or even in one bed, one person is full of tribulation and heartache because of a burdened conscience, while the other is full of comfort and joy. Yet neither can take from or give to the other what is his; each must remain as he is understood by himself and by God. Let everyone look to his own conscience, for therein the great chasm is found: between light and darkness, between salvation <term>salvation: "Seligkeit," a state of eternal spiritual blessedness</term> and damnation.

Thirdly, we must also recognize and confess from this conversation between the two persons in that world that the pleasure, joy, and delight of the unbelievers in this world is only external and very brief. For the world, or worldly life with its lusts, passes away quickly, and inwardly in the heart there is no rest. Conversely, the brief persecution and tribulation of the believers is, on the outside, quite short and slight. But inwardly they keep the peace of Christ. Therefore, the suffering of this time, which is light and slight, is not worthy of the glory and joys that shall be revealed in—

### Concerning the Rich Man and Poor Lazarus.

<sig>P ij</sig>

of the coming world in the believers. Just as, on the other hand, the joy and delight or pleasure of the unbelievers is not worthy of <note>That is, cannot be compared to the scale of</note> the damnation and hell-torment that must be revealed in the damned in that world. O, would to GOD the preachers believed such things; they would rouse themselves and others to repentance <note>original German: "Busse," which carries the sense of both internal change and outward penance</note>. The godless, because of their evil and unclean conscience, begin their hell in this world. For they live and walk in the very thing that they carry within them, just as the righteous begin their salvation <note>original: "Seligkeit"</note> in this world.

**Fourthly**, this conversation also testifies that all things shall be honestly repaid.

O you righteous GOD, you remain righteous in eternity; you do no wrong to anyone. What each person has earned on earth during their bodily life, that same thing he receives, be it good or evil; and with whatever someone sins, with that same thing shall he be tortured and punished. For their works follow them—which the preachers <note>original: "Prædicanten," likely referring to the established Lutheran or Reformed clergy</note> do not want to believe. They talk the common man out of it and write against it, saying it is false and wrong to say much about the other world or how it goes for this person or that. Yes, even those caught up in ecstasy <note>original: "Entzückten," referring to people who have had visions or near-death experiences</note> on their deathbeds, if they are called back to life, are supposedly to reveal or say nothing at all about the blessed and the damned whom they have seen in their trance; nor should they exhort the people to repentance. As I myself heard from one such preacher, who was angry at such people and said: "What should we living preachers do with our wives and children? Where would we stay, how would we support ourselves, if the common man were to listen to such people? They would fall away from us and give us no more salary <note>original: "Besoldung"</note>." Therefore, such people should not preach repentance; it is not commanded of them (he said). But I well believe that he and his pack shall soon experience what and how they preached the Gospel <note>original: "Evangelium"</note> for repentance in this world. So perverted are the preachers that they want to hear nothing at all of the other world, nor allow those who have seen it themselves to speak of it.

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the interpretation of the Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, transitioning from a discussion of divine justice and the afterlife to the specific moral failings and "fruits of unbelief" demonstrated by the rich man in his earthly life.</meta>

# A Sermon

**Fifthly**, we see and recognize from this conversation that in this temporal life, two paths are set before every person in which they may walk: one is the path in Adam according to flesh and blood, following one's own lust and will—this path leads, with the rich man, toward eternal darkness and hellish torment. The other is the path in Christ according to the new birth, following God’s law and will—this path leads, with poor Lazarus, toward eternal rest and joy. Thus, it remains true: God the Lord planted the tree of knowledge of good and evil in the middle of the garden and said: "You shall not eat from it; do not let yourself lust after it; you shall not desire it," etc.

In this conversation or text, we also understand the causes of why the upright, pious man was condemned, and why Lazarus—who was rejected and despised by the world and undoubtedly also branded a heretic <note>original: "verketzert." In the 17th century, radical spiritualists often complained that the established church labeled any true seeker a heretic.</note> (since almost all Christians are branded heretics)—was saved. We establish the primary cause of the rich man’s damnation as unbelief, which makes itself known through its nature and its fruits. 

Firstly, look at his clothing and how it was chosen out of pride <term>Hoffart: a specific type of arrogant, social pride or vanity often expressed through luxury</term>; he dressed himself in purple and costly linen, which kings were accustomed to wear. He and his wife, his sons and daughters, carried themselves as if they were noblemen, as if they were princes or counts. Who has the time to recount what finery, pride, and splendor they displayed on a feast day when they were meant to go to church?

The second kind of unbelief was the excess of food and drink; he lived every day magnificently and in joy, carousing in companies, and had his food served to him with the accompaniment of musical instruments. He ate without any hunger and drank without thirst. He was never truly alert nor sober <note>original: "Ward nie wahn noch nüchtern." The word "wahn" here likely refers to a state of being "fully present" or "sane," suggesting the rich man lived in a constant daze of intoxicated luxury.</note>; this lasted every day, where there had to be pipes, harps, and various musical instruments <note>original Latin: "Instrumenta der Musica"</note>. Man is created only for bare necessity—to sustain his mortal body with food and drink—yet he practices terrible pride with these things, and with great excess.

<summary>The author identifies two spiritual paths—fleshly and spiritual—and argues that the rich man's damnation resulted from the "fruits of unbelief," specifically his vanity in dress and his gluttonous, intoxicated lifestyle.</summary>

<keywords>two paths, Adam and Christ, Tree of Knowledge, unbelief, pride, luxury, gluttony, social status, heresy</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the rich man’s "fruits of unbelief," moving from his outward vanity in clothing and status to his distractions, his superficial religious life, and ultimately his hollow deathbed confession.</meta>

->**concerning the rich man and poor Lazarus.**<-

**Thirdly**, he also devoted himself to various forms of amusement, such as jousting, racing, hunting, and manifold pastimes. Thus, year in and year out, no thought was given to Christ’s teaching, life, or conduct, nor to His death or His cross. For this reason, he also forgot the poor; even if he did have his servants give alms <note>original: *Eleemosynam*</note>, it did not proceed from love. Before the world, he was upright, honest, and pious; he gladly went to church, to confession, and to the sacrament, but without any true repentance <term>Busse: deep internal repentance and a change of heart, contrasted here with the mere outward ritual of penance</term> or fear of God. For his heart was entirely alienated from the life that is of God in Christ Jesus. That is to say, he knew nothing at all of the life of Christ; perhaps he had never even heard of it from the pulpit, because of his "soup-eaters" <note>original: *Suppenessern*. A derogatory term for sycophants or clergy who flatter a wealthy patron for the sake of a free meal.</note> who were around him and with him daily.

For he was a friend and patron to all the clergy and preachers <note>original: *Prædicanten*</note>, because they were in complete agreement with his lifestyle and did not trouble his conscience. This pleasure, joy, glory, splendor, and his church-going continued without any repentance, remorse, or sorrow over his sins until he was confined to his bed and fell ill. When he could no longer indulge the lusts of his flesh—which caused him sincere sorrow—he summoned his priests to him. He made his acknowledgment and confession <note>original: *Beichte*</note>, speaking with his mouth but not from his heart: "Dear sir, I am a sinner; yet as you yourself know, I have gladly gone to church, have confessed, and received the sacrament. But now I can go no further; I am sorry that I shall live no longer." (He should have said: "I am sorry that I have lived in such sins.") 

But the priest comforted him with beautiful verses from the Holy Scriptures, telling him he should think of Christ’s death, etc. And he absolved him of his sins, even though his conscience had not been drawn by God toward true repentance. It was merely a human repentance, a human confession, an absolution of the mouth and the hand <note>That is, a ritual performed only through speech and the physical gesture of blessing, lacking spiritual substance.</note>. Yet, before the world and the whole city, it had such an appearance of holiness—especially since the clergy kept watch during the night of his passing—that no one dared say this man stood poorly before God. Finally, he was buried magnificently and at great cost; the bishop and all the preachers...

<summary>This page describes the rich man’s superficial piety and his reliance on "soup-eater" clergy who affirmed his lifestyle rather than calling him to repentance. It concludes with his hollow deathbed confession and a grand funeral that masked his true spiritual state from the world.</summary>

<keywords>hypocrisy, soup-eaters, false repentance, absolution, clergy, funeral rites, spiritual alienation</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the rich man's spiritual state, specifically addressing the disparity between his grand public funeral and his true state in the afterlife, contrasted with the quiet, uncelebrated death of the beggar Lazarus.</meta>

# A Sermon /

[They] even went along with him with torches, and a beautiful funeral sermon was preached for him, [praising] how well he had lived and how godly he had died. But hear what the Lord says here, He who sees into both this world and the next: *He was buried.* While he was now in hell and in torment. Who would dare say such a thing? If anyone in this day and age wished to speak of the next world and say that this Prince, Emperor, Nobleman, Count, Citizen, or Peasant was in Hell, his relatives would take Christ Himself to court and spend all their possessions and property on lawsuits, saying: "How dare you say such things? He was indeed a pious, upright, and honorable man; <term>piè vixit, piè morixit: he lived piously, he died piously</term>, and he made quite a beautiful end." And then this fanatic <note>original: *Schwermer*—a derogatory term often used in the Reformation era for religious radicals or agitators who claimed direct inspiration</note> dares to say: "Our cousin, our friend, our dear uncle is in Hell!" [They would say:] "Look at how he lived, how he died, and how he was so magnificently escorted to his burial!" But the end of his unbelief is darkness and damnation.

In contrast, poor Lazarus stood firm in faith, love, patience, and hope. He was despised by the world. While he was still healthy and strong, he went to hear the sermon and conducted himself like others in obedience to the Church; he sought his nourishment at the doors. Finally, he came to the hospital <note>In the 16th century, a "hospital" was more of a poorhouse or hospice for the destitute than a modern medical facility</note>. And when he was about to die, there was no <term>Prædicant: preacher or minister</term> around him to comfort him with beautiful verses from the Holy Scripture. [But] he held these [scriptures] for himself in his heart. There was no one to whom he might have confessed; he had Christ as his Confessor and Absolver within himself. And although he died without the Sacrament, he was nevertheless not rejected by God; he had practiced this often during his healthy life and had become one with Christ, in whom he walked, prayed, and also died. Even though (I say) he died without the presence of the clergy, the angels nevertheless carried him into Abraham's bosom. No one walked with him to his grave, nor was any funeral sermon preached for him, for there was no money or shroud <note>original: *Leichttuch*—the ceremonial pall used to cover a coffin</note> to be expected. Lazarus had lived miserably in the eyes of the world, and he died and was buried miserably in the eyes of the world, without the tolling of bells, without singing, without the ringing [of chimes]. But the ri-

<meta>catchword: -ch [completing "reiche" or "rich man"]</meta>

<summary>This page contrasts the grand, socially-celebrated funeral of the rich man with the lonely, ignored death of Lazarus, arguing that external religious rituals and social status do not reflect one's true standing before God. It emphasizes that Lazarus had internal faith and Christ as his "confessor," even without the presence of clergy or formal sacraments at his death.</summary>

<keywords>Rich Man and Lazarus, funeral sermon, salvation, internal faith, Prædicant, Abraham's bosom, social hypocrisy, deathbed confession</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page concludes the sermon regarding the rich man and the beggar Lazarus, continuing from the description of the rich man's elaborate funeral preparations on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>About the rich man and poor Lazarus. 119</header>

[This] rich man lived magnificently before the world; he also died magnificently and was buried honorably, with people, singing, and the ringing of bells; with a great procession of the whole city, adorned with a beautiful sermon—for he had gold guilders <note>The *Goldgulden* was a high-value gold coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire.</note> to give the preacher, and a length of London cloth <note>original: *Lündisch Tuch*—a high-quality, expensive wool fabric imported from London, often given as a prestigious gift or payment.</note>. Thus, a person might almost be considered blessed simply because of such a spiritual crowd, the singing, and the pomp.

But the Lord tells us explicitly that he was in Hell and in torment; we should believe this as well. And Lazarus was in the bosom of Abraham <note>A biblical expression referring to the place of comfort in the afterlife where the righteous await judgment.</note>. Oh, how brief is the pleasure and willful indulgence of wicked men in this world! In contrast, their pain and the torture of Hell are eternal without end. Oh, how brief is the Christians' cross, anxiety, and sorrow—but their rest, joy, and glory are eternal without end. Therefore, we should strive to walk the path of God in Christ, in contempt, scorn, persecution, and in all patience; then we will not regret that which we have suffered on earth.

->THE END.<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut tailpiece in an inverted triangular shape, featuring symmetrical floral and scrollwork designs with stylized foliage.</image-desc>

<summary>This page concludes the contrast between the rich man’s earthly pomp and his spiritual damnation, emphasizing that a grand funeral cannot change one's fate in the afterlife. It encourages believers to endure earthly suffering, as the rewards of heaven are eternal compared to the fleeting pleasures of the world.</summary>

<keywords>Lazarus, the Rich Man, Gold Guilders, London Cloth, Torments of Hell, Bosom of Abraham, Christ, Patience in Suffering, Eternal Life</keywords>

<summary>This title page introduces a primary work by the German mystic Valentin Weigel, centered on the philosophical and spiritual mandate to "know thyself." It defines the human being as a microcosm—a "little world"—that contains the essence of the entire cosmos and the divine within.</summary>
